I mean, I don’t love the idea of looking nervous in front of a client, obviously—barbering is such a face-to-face job, and people notice your hands, your confidence, the whole vibe of the shop.\n\nBut… I don’t know, when it happens, I’m not mostly thinking, “They’re going to judge me.” It’s more just the situation itself gets in my head. Like the razor and what I’m doing with it.\n\nThough sometimes I do wonder if they can tell I’m tense, especially if the shop is quiet. My old boss used to say clients can feel when you’re not relaxed—he had all these sayings about “the chair being a stage” and stuff. Sorry, I’m wandering. But no, I don’t think it’s mainly about people thinking badly of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your fear about using the straight razor is excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, part of me knows it’s excessive. I trained with straight razors, it’s literally just one of the tools of the trade. I can use clippers all day, scissors, trimmers, no problem—half the time I’m not even thinking about it.\n\nBut when that straight razor is sitting there open at my station, it doesn’t feel like “just a tool.” It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself badly. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, because barbers use them every day, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: Outside of using or thinking about the straight razor at work, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about many other things in your life?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about normal stuff, like whether a client’s going to like the cut, or bills, or if I’m running late—normal life stuff.\n\nBut it’s not like my brain is constantly jumping from one worry to the next all day. It’s more tied to the razor thing. Like if I’m not at work or not thinking about using it, I’m usually not sitting around worrying about a bunch of other things.\n\nAlthough sometimes work in general gets in my head—like the shop atmosphere, keeping your station clean, people watching you cut hair. There was this one guy I used to work with who could talk nonstop while doing a fade perfectly, and I was always like, how do you even do that? Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I worry a lot about many other things. Mostly it’s that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid you might lose control and intentionally hurt a client with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid I’ll choose to do something on purpose. It’s not that I want to hurt anyone or that I think I’m going to suddenly decide to.\n\nIt’s more like, when the straight razor is open there on my station, my brain just goes straight to, “What if I hurt them?” or “What if I cut myself badly?” Even though I know I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking. It’s just that razor—seeing it open makes the whole thing feel dangerous in a way I know doesn’t totally make sense.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid using the straight razor, does that make your fear go down right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. Like, if I find a way not to use it, I feel relief pretty much right away. I’ll switch clients if I can, or say the blade’s missing, or just kind of keep it tucked away in the drawer and hope nobody asks for a shave.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it’s part of the job, you know? In barber school they make it seem like this classic skill, and some guys act like it’s the whole identity of being a barber—hot towel, straight razor, the whole thing. I used to think that looked really cool, actually.\n\nBut now, if I avoid it, my body calms down fast. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m just… shaking through the whole shave, trying to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Are you mainly afraid of the straight razor itself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mainly the straight razor itself, I guess. Like, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it. It’s not like I’m scared of every sharp thing—I mean, I use clippers all day, scissors too, and that’s fine.\n\nBut when that straight razor is sitting open at my station… it just changes. My brain goes, like, “What if I hurt somebody?” or “What if I cut myself really badly?” Even if nothing is happening yet.\n\nIt’s weird because some barbers make it look so smooth, like it’s this elegant old-school thing, and I used to think that was kind of cool. But for me now, once it’s open, it feels dangerous in a way I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about accidentally cutting someone ever come into your mind when you’re away from the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I mean, not really in the same way, I don’t think.\n\nLike if I’m at home making food and I see a knife, I don’t usually spiral about a client or anything. Although I did cut myself once chopping onions, but that was just me being stupid and rushing, not like… the barber thing.\n\nIt’s more tied to the shop, I guess, and that razor being there. But I don’t know, sometimes if I know I have a client coming in who might ask for a shave, I’ll start thinking about it before they even get there. So… away from the actual razor? Maybe a little if I’m anticipating it, but it’s not like I’m walking around all day thinking about cutting people.\n\nDoctor: When the thought comes up that you might cut someone, do you mentally replay the shave steps to reassure yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… mentally replay the steps? I don’t know if I do it like that exactly.\n\nLike, I’ll think, “Okay, don’t mess up, keep your hand steady,” but I’m not sure I’m going through the whole shave in my head like a checklist or anything. Barber school drilled those steps into us so much—stretch the skin, angle the blade, light pressure—and our instructor used to walk around correcting everybody like a drill sergeant. He had this old mustache too, kind of like one of those 1920s barbershop posters…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed myself doing a full mental replay to feel better. It’s more just the fear pops up and I’m trying to get through it or avoid it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

It’s more just the fear pops up and I’m trying to get through it or avoid it.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you’re holding the straight razor, do you ever get a sudden unwanted image or urge of slashing or intentionally hurting the client?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 27 / barber / graduate\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t think so? Not like an urge, no. That sounds… I don’t know, really scary to even say.

When I’m holding it, my head is more like, “Don’t slip, don’t mess this up,” and then I notice my hand or the angle, or the client moving a tiny bit. Like some guys talk the whole time in the chair, you know, and they’ll turn their head while they’re telling you about their weekend, and I’m like, please just stay still for two seconds.

But an image of me slashing someone on purpose… I haven’t noticed that. It’s more just fear something could go wrong, if that makes sense.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.298, F42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess it’s work. I’m a barber, and it’s gotten to this point where I’ll do almost anything to not use the straight razor. Like I’ll switch clients with someone, or say the blade’s missing, or just hide it in the drawer, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nAnd I mean, the shop is busy, there’s always music on, guys talking about basketball or whatever, so sometimes I can kind of cover it. But if I can’t get out of doing the shave, I’m shaking through the whole thing. That’s basically why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: When you have to use the straight razor, do you get unwanted thoughts that you might accidentally cut or seriously hurt the client?\n\nPatient: Yeah… that’s pretty much it. I know, logically, it’s just a tool. I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking about it—like I’m fine doing fades, lineups, all that.\n\nBut when the straight razor is open at my station, it’s like my brain jumps straight to, “I’m going to cut this guy,” or cut myself badly. And then I start thinking about how close it is to someone’s neck, which is stupid because that’s literally the job, you know? But it feels like something bad is about to happen.\n\nDoctor: When that thought comes up, does it feel like an unwanted thought popping into your mind, even though you don’t want to hurt anyone?\n\nPatient: Yeah, yeah, it’s unwanted. I mean, I don’t want to hurt anybody—obviously. I cut hair because I like making people look good, you know? Some guys come in before weddings or job interviews, and they’re trusting you, and then my head goes off somewhere else.\n\nBut yeah, when that straight razor is open at my station, even though I know it’s just a tool I trained with, it suddenly feels like I’m about to cut the client badly or even cut myself. Clippers don’t do that to me at all. It’s just that razor.\n\nDoctor: When that thought shows up, does it feel intrusive and against what you want, like it pops into your mind even though you don’t want it there?\n\nPatient: I mean… I think so? It’s hard to explain. It’s not like I’m standing there planning anything, if that’s what you mean. It just kind of jumps in, and then I get stuck on it.\n\nLike, even yesterday, this guy was talking to me about his kid’s soccer game while I was doing his neck cleanup, and I’m supposed to be listening, but then I’m suddenly thinking about the razor and how sharp it is, and then I’m not really hearing him anymore. Which is awkward because customers can tell when you’re not paying attention.\n\nSo yeah, I guess it feels like it pops in when I don’t want it to. I don’t know if “intrusive” is the right word, but it’s definitely not something I’m trying to think about.\n\nDoctor: When that fear comes up, do you find yourself repeatedly checking the razor to make sure it’s safe?\n\nPatient: Um… checking it repeatedly? I don’t know. I mean, I look at it, obviously, because I have to use it and make sure it’s set up right, but I don’t know if I’d call it checking over and over to make sure it’s safe.\n\nSometimes I’ll kind of stare at it longer than I need to, like I’m trying to convince myself it’s just a razor, you know? But then I’ll get distracted thinking about how old-school barbers used to use these things all day like it was nothing—my uncle used to talk about that, actually, he had this whole thing about “real barbering” and keeping the blade angle right.\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess… I haven’t really noticed myself repeatedly checking it in a specific way. It’s more that once I see it, I get tense about using it.\n\nDoctor: Do you only get this fear when the straight razor is there in front of you?\n\nPatient: Not only when it’s right in front of me, no. Like, the second a client asks for a razor shave, I already feel it start—my chest tightens up, my hands get sweaty, and it’s like my body wants to step back before I’ve even done anything.\n\nAnd then if I actually see the blade flipped open, it’s worse. Which is annoying, because some clients ask for it like it’s this classic barber thing, you know, like they’ve seen it in movies or those fancy shops with the hot towels and everything. I used to think that part of the job was kind of cool, honestly.\n\nBut now, yeah, even hearing them ask can set it off.\n\nDoctor: When that thought about cutting someone comes up, do you do anything on purpose to make the thought go away or feel less dangerous?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Like, I don’t have a specific thing I do in my head, if that’s what you mean. I don’t say a phrase or count or anything like that.\n\nSometimes I’ll try to focus really hard on the actual steps, like the angle and where my hand is supposed to be, because that’s what they drilled into us when I was learning. My instructor used to make this big deal about posture too, like your elbow position mattered more than people think—sorry, that’s probably not what you asked.\n\nI guess I just try to push it away or focus on the job, but I don’t know if I’m doing something on purpose to make it “less dangerous.” It’s more like I’m uncomfortable and I’m trying to get through the moment.\n\nDoctor: When this happens at work, are you mainly afraid that other people will notice you seem anxious or think badly of you?\n\nPatient: I don’t think that’s the main thing, no. I mean, I don’t love the idea of looking nervous in front of a client, obviously—barbering is such a face-to-face job, and people notice your hands, your confidence, the whole vibe of the shop.\n\nBut… I don’t know, when it happens, I’m not mostly thinking, “They’re going to judge me.” It’s more just the situation itself gets in my head. Like the razor and what I’m doing with it.\n\nThough sometimes I do wonder if they can tell I’m tense, especially if the shop is quiet. My old boss used to say clients can feel when you’re not relaxed—he had all these sayings about “the chair being a stage” and stuff. Sorry, I’m wandering. But no, I don’t think it’s mainly about people thinking badly of me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel that your fear about using the straight razor is excessive or unreasonable?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, part of me knows it’s excessive. I trained with straight razors, it’s literally just one of the tools of the trade. I can use clippers all day, scissors, trimmers, no problem—half the time I’m not even thinking about it.\n\nBut when that straight razor is sitting there open at my station, it doesn’t feel like “just a tool.” It feels like I’m about to hurt somebody, or like I’m going to cut myself badly. And I know that probably sounds dramatic, because barbers use them every day, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: Outside of using or thinking about the straight razor at work, do you also find yourself worrying a lot about many other things in your life?\n\nPatient: Um… not really, I don’t think so. I mean, I worry about normal stuff, like whether a client’s going to like the cut, or bills, or if I’m running late—normal life stuff.\n\nBut it’s not like my brain is constantly jumping from one worry to the next all day. It’s more tied to the razor thing. Like if I’m not at work or not thinking about using it, I’m usually not sitting around worrying about a bunch of other things.\n\nAlthough sometimes work in general gets in my head—like the shop atmosphere, keeping your station clean, people watching you cut hair. There was this one guy I used to work with who could talk nonstop while doing a fade perfectly, and I was always like, how do you even do that? Sorry, that’s not really what you asked.\n\nSo… no, I wouldn’t say I worry a lot about many other things. Mostly it’s that specific situation.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid you might lose control and intentionally hurt a client with the straight razor?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think it’s like I’m afraid I’ll choose to do something on purpose. It’s not that I want to hurt anyone or that I think I’m going to suddenly decide to.\n\nIt’s more like, when the straight razor is open there on my station, my brain just goes straight to, “What if I hurt them?” or “What if I cut myself badly?” Even though I know I trained with it, and I can use clippers all day without even thinking. It’s just that razor—seeing it open makes the whole thing feel dangerous in a way I know doesn’t totally make sense.\n\nDoctor: When you avoid using the straight razor, does that make your fear go down right away?\n\nPatient: Yeah… honestly, yes. Like, if I find a way not to use it, I feel relief pretty much right away. I’ll switch clients if I can, or say the blade’s missing, or just kind of keep it tucked away in the drawer and hope nobody asks for a shave.\n\nIt’s embarrassing, because it’s part of the job, you know? In barber school they make it seem like this classic skill, and some guys act like it’s the whole identity of being a barber—hot towel, straight razor, the whole thing. I used to think that looked really cool, actually.\n\nBut now, if I avoid it, my body calms down fast. And if I can’t get out of it, I’m just… shaking through the whole shave, trying to look normal.\n\nDoctor: Are you mainly afraid of the straight razor itself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… mainly the straight razor itself, I guess. Like, I know it’s just a tool, and I trained with it. It’s not like I’m scared of every sharp thing—I mean, I use clippers all day, scissors too, and that’s fine.\n\nBut when that straight razor is sitting open at my station… it just changes. My brain goes, like, “What if I hurt somebody?” or “What if I cut myself really badly?” Even if nothing is happening yet.\n\nIt’s weird because some barbers make it look so smooth, like it’s this elegant old-school thing, and I used to think that was kind of cool. But for me now, once it’s open, it feels dangerous in a way I can’t shake.\n\nDoctor: Do thoughts about accidentally cutting someone ever come into your mind when you’re away from the straight razor?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure… I mean, not really in the same way, I don’t think.\n\nLike if I’m at home making food and I see a knife, I don’t usually spiral about a client or anything. Although I did cut myself once chopping onions, but that was just me being stupid and rushing, not like… the barber thing.\n\nIt’s more tied to the shop, I guess, and that razor being there. But I don’t know, sometimes if I know I have a client coming in who might ask for a shave, I’ll start thinking about it before they even get there. So… away from the actual razor? Maybe a little if I’m anticipating it, but it’s not like I’m walking around all day thinking about cutting people.\n\nDoctor: When the thought comes up that you might cut someone, do you mentally replay the shave steps to reassure yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… mentally replay the steps? I don’t know if I do it like that exactly.\n\nLike, I’ll think, “Okay, don’t mess up, keep your hand steady,” but I’m not sure I’m going through the whole shave in my head like a checklist or anything. Barber school drilled those steps into us so much—stretch the skin, angle the blade, light pressure—and our instructor used to walk around correcting everybody like a drill sergeant. He had this old mustache too, kind of like one of those 1920s barbershop posters…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed myself doing a full mental replay to feel better. It’s more just the fear pops up and I’m trying to get through it or avoid it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re holding the straight razor, do you ever get a sudden unwanted image or urge of slashing or intentionally hurting the client?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Not like an urge, no. That sounds… I don’t know, really scary to even say.\n\nWhen I’m holding it, my head is more like, “Don’t slip, don’t mess this up,” and then I notice my hand or the angle, or the client moving a tiny bit. Like some guys talk the whole time in the chair, you know, and they’ll turn their head while they’re telling you about their weekend, and I’m like, please just stay still for two seconds.\n\nBut an image of me slashing someone on purpose… I haven’t noticed that. It’s more just fear something could go wrong, if that makes sense.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Repeated checking, rituals, broader obsessions, and ego-dystonic aggressive urges/images are not supported, making OCD less likely.","is_final":true}

Maybe a little if I’m anticipating it, but it’s not like I’m walking around all day thinking about cutting people.\n\nDoctor: When the thought comes up that you might cut someone, do you mentally replay the shave steps to reassure yourself?\n\nPatient: Um… mentally replay the steps? I don’t know if I do it like that exactly.\n\nLike, I’ll think, “Okay, don’t mess up, keep your hand steady,” but I’m not sure I’m going through the whole shave in my head like a checklist or anything. Barber school drilled those steps into us so much—stretch the skin, angle the blade, light pressure—and our instructor used to walk around correcting everybody like a drill sergeant. He had this old mustache too, kind of like one of those 1920s barbershop posters…\n\nSorry, I’m getting off track. I guess I’m not sure. I haven’t really noticed myself doing a full mental replay to feel better. It’s more just the fear pops up and I’m trying to get through it or avoid it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re holding the straight razor, do you ever get a sudden unwanted image or urge of slashing or intentionally hurting the client?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t think so? Not like an urge, no. That sounds… I don’t know, really scary to even say.\n\nWhen I’m holding it, my head is more like, “Don’t slip, don’t mess this up,” and then I notice my hand or the angle, or the client moving a tiny bit. Like some guys talk the whole time in the chair, you know, and they’ll turn their head while they’re telling you about their weekend, and I’m like, please just stay still for two seconds.\n\nBut an image of me slashing someone on purpose… I haven’t noticed that. It’s more just fear something could go wrong, if that makes sense."
